Exodus cross-references

Exodus 1:1–14:31 · 388 completed verses · 777 completed pairs

Initial curated pass. Next: Exodus 15:1. Later source verses remain pending. Greek-based Saints-Translation numbering is retained.

Completed means the initial curated cross-reference pass: the source verse and every listed target passage were read in the pinned Saints-Translation English records, and each retained pair has a specific explanation. It does not mean every possible biblical connection has been exhausted, or independent scholarly approval.

Exodus 1:1

These are the names of the sons of Israel who entered Egypt with Jacob their father; each came with his whole household:

  1. Genesis 46:8 Explicit migration recollection

    Genesis introduces the names of Israel's sons entering Egypt with Jacob; Exodus resumes that same household migration with a corresponding introductory formula.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 46:8 And these are the names of the sons of Israel who entered into Egypt, Jacob and his sons. Jacob’s firstborn was Reuben.

    Pinned source record

  2. Acts 7:14 Explicit retrospective account

    Stephen describes Joseph summoning Jacob and his relatives into Egypt, supplying the family movement that Exodus's opening household list recalls.

    Read matching passage

    Acts 7:14 Joseph sent and summoned his father Jacob and all his relatives, seventy-five persons in all.

    Pinned source record

Exodus 1:2

Reuben, Simeon, Levi, and Judah;

  1. Genesis 35:23 Genealogical correspondence

    Reuben, Simeon, Levi and Judah appear together among Leah's sons, identifying the four family heads named in the opening Egyptian household list.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 35:23 The sons of Leah: Reuben, Jacob’s firstborn, Simeon, Levi, Judah, Issachar, and Zebulun.

    Pinned source record

  2. Genesis 46:8-12 Explicit migration genealogy

    The earlier migration catalogue identifies Reuben, Simeon, Levi and Judah and their family branches, supplying the Egyptian-entry genealogy summarized by these four names.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 46:8 And these are the names of the sons of Israel who entered into Egypt, Jacob and his sons. Jacob’s firstborn was Reuben.

    Genesis 46:9 And Reuben’s sons: Hanoch, Pallu, Hezron, and Carmi.

    Genesis 46:10 And Simeon’s sons: Jemuel, Jamin, Aod, Jachin, Saar, and Shaul the son of the Canaanite woman.

    Genesis 46:11 And Levi’s sons: Gershon, Kohath, and Merari.

    Genesis 46:12 And Judah’s sons: Er, Onan, Shelah, Perez, and Zerah. But Er and Onan died in the land of Canaan. And Perez’s sons were Hezron and Jemuel.

    Pinned source record

Exodus 1:3

Issachar, Zebulun, and Benjamin;

  1. Genesis 35:23-24 Genealogical correspondence

    Issachar and Zebulun are listed as Leah's sons and Benjamin as Rachel's son, identifying the three named brothers without imposing a different ordering on Exodus.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 35:23 The sons of Leah: Reuben, Jacob’s firstborn, Simeon, Levi, Judah, Issachar, and Zebulun.

    Genesis 35:24 And the sons of Rachel: Joseph and Benjamin.

    Pinned source record

  2. Genesis 46:13-14 Genealogical continuation

    The migration catalogue records Issachar's and Zebulun's sons, supplying the household branches of the first two family heads named here.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 46:13 And Issachar’s sons: Tola, Phua, Jasoub, and Zambram.

    Genesis 46:14 And Zebulun’s sons: Sered, Allon, and Aloel.

    Pinned source record

  3. Genesis 46:21 Genealogical continuation

    The earlier migration catalogue supplies Benjamin's descendants, continuing the household branch of the third brother named here while retaining its own expanded family wording.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 46:21 And Benjamin’s sons: Bala, Chobor, and Ashbel. And Bala’s sons were Gera, Noeman, Anchis, Ros, Mamphin, and Ophimin; and Gera begot Arad.

    Pinned source record

Exodus 1:4

Dan and Naphtali, Gad and Asher.

  1. Genesis 35:25-26 Genealogical correspondence

    Dan and Naphtali are Bilhah's sons, while Gad and Asher are Zilpah's, identifying the four brothers in Exodus without confusing their mothers.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 35:25 And the sons of Bilhah, Rachel’s slave woman: Dan and Naphtali.

    Genesis 35:26 And the sons of Zilpah, Leah’s slave woman: Gad and Asher. These are Jacob’s sons, who were born to him in Mesopotamia of Syria.

    Pinned source record

  2. Genesis 46:16-18 Genealogical continuation

    The migration catalogue develops Gad's and Asher's family branches and identifies Zilpah's descendants, supplying household context for these two names.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 46:16 And Gad’s sons: Saphon, Haggis, Saunis, Thasoban, Aedis, Aroedis, and Aroelis.

    Genesis 46:17 And Asher’s sons: Jemna, Jesoua, Jeul, and Baria, and Sara their sister. And Baria’s sons: Chobor and Melchiel.

    Genesis 46:18 These are the sons of Zilpah, whom Laban gave to Leah his daughter; and she bore these to Jacob, sixteen souls.

    Pinned source record

  3. Genesis 46:23-25 Genealogical continuation

    Dan's and Naphtali's descendants appear in the earlier migration catalogue under Bilhah's family summary, supplying the branches of the other two brothers named here.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 46:23 And the sons of Dan: Asom.

    Genesis 46:24 And Naphtali’s sons: Asiel, Gouni, Issaar, and Syllem.

    Genesis 46:25 These are the sons of Bilhah, whom Laban gave to Rachel his daughter; and she bore these to Jacob: all the souls were seven.

    Pinned source record

Exodus 1:5

Now Joseph was in Egypt; and all the souls descended from Jacob were seventy-five.

  1. Genesis 46:27 Explicit migration-total correspondence

    Genesis gives seventy-five for Jacob's Egyptian household and identifies Joseph's Egyptian-born descendants; Exodus retains seventy-five while noting Joseph's prior presence there.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 46:27 And Joseph’s sons who were born to him in the land of Egypt were nine souls. All the souls of Jacob’s house who entered into Egypt were seventy-five.

    Pinned source record

  2. Acts 7:14 Explicit retrospective total

    Stephen likewise gives seventy-five relatives in Joseph's summons of Jacob's family, matching the supplied Exodus total without replacing it with another edition's count.

    Read matching passage

    Acts 7:14 Joseph sent and summoned his father Jacob and all his relatives, seventy-five persons in all.

    Pinned source record

  3. Deuteronomy 10:22 Related migration account with different totals

    Deuteronomy recalls the fathers entering Egypt with seventy souls, whereas this Exodus text says seventy-five; the common migration setting is compared with both stated totals preserved.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 10:22 Your fathers went down into Egypt with seventy souls; but now the Lord your God has made you as the stars of heaven in multitude.

    Pinned source record

Exodus 1:6

And Joseph died, and all his brothers, and all that generation.

  1. Genesis 50:26 Explicit narrative continuation

    Genesis closes with Joseph's death and burial rites in Egypt; Exodus includes his death in its notice that the earlier family generation has passed away.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 50:26 And Joseph died, being a hundred and ten years old; and they performed his burial rites, and laid him in a coffin in Egypt.

    Pinned source record

  2. Acts 7:15 Explicit retrospective account

    Stephen summarizes Jacob's descent to Egypt and the deaths of Jacob and the fathers, corresponding to the passing of the ancestral generation narrated here.

    Read matching passage

    Acts 7:15 Jacob went down to Egypt, and he and our fathers died.

    Pinned source record

Exodus 1:7

And the sons of Israel increased and multiplied, and became numerous and exceedingly, exceedingly strong; and the land multiplied them.

  1. Genesis 46:3 Promise and narrative development

    God had promised to make Jacob a great nation in Egypt; the multiplication and strength of Israel's descendants develop that promise in the migration's setting.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 46:3 And He said, “I am the God of your fathers. Do not fear to go down into Egypt, for I will make you a great nation there.

    Pinned source record

  2. Psalms 104:24 Explicit retrospective account

    The psalm recounts God's greatly increasing His people and making them stronger than their enemies, recalling the population growth and strength narrated here.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 104:24 He greatly increased His people and made them stronger than their enemies.

    Pinned source record

  3. Acts 7:17 Explicit retrospective account

    Stephen expressly recalls the people's growth and multiplication in Egypt as the time of the promise approached, summarizing the development stated here.

    Read matching passage

    Acts 7:17 As the time of the promise God had declared to Abraham drew near, the people grew and multiplied in Egypt,

    Pinned source record

Exodus 1:8

And another king arose over Egypt, who did not know Joseph.

  1. Acts 7:18 Explicit retrospective quotation

    Stephen repeats the arising of another Egyptian king who did not know Joseph, retaining the distinctive transition that introduces Israel's oppression.

    Read matching passage

    Acts 7:18 until another king arose over Egypt, who did not know Joseph.

    Pinned source record

  2. Psalms 104:25 Related retrospective transition

    The psalm moves from Israel's increase to hostility and deceit against God's servants, corresponding to the changed Egyptian treatment following the new king's appearance without supplying his historical name.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 104:25 He turned their heart to hate His people, to deal deceitfully with His servants.

    Pinned source record

Exodus 1:9

And he said to his nation, “Behold, the people of the sons of Israel are a great multitude, and are stronger than we are.

  1. Exodus 1:7 Same-chapter narrated growth and royal response

    The narrator has described Israel's great increase and strength; the king now presents that population as stronger than his own people, keeping his comparative claim within his speech.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 1:7 And the sons of Israel increased and multiplied, and became numerous and exceedingly, exceedingly strong; and the land multiplied them.

    Pinned source record

  2. Psalms 104:24 Explicit retrospective population theme

    The psalm's account of God's people becoming stronger than their enemies corresponds to the strength the Egyptian king invokes as grounds for alarm.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 104:24 He greatly increased His people and made them stronger than their enemies.

    Pinned source record

Exodus 1:10

Come, therefore, let us deal craftily with them, lest they multiply; and when war befalls us, they also join our adversaries, wage war against us, and leave the land.”

  1. Psalms 104:25 Explicit retrospective hostility

    The psalm describes hatred of God's people and deceitful treatment of His servants, corresponding to the king's proposed crafty policy against Israel.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 104:25 He turned their heart to hate His people, to deal deceitfully with His servants.

    Pinned source record

  2. Acts 7:19 Explicit retrospective account

    Stephen recounts the king's crafty mistreatment and exposure of infants, summarizing the policy whose proposed purpose here is to prevent Israel's further growth.

    Read matching passage

    Acts 7:19 He dealt craftily with our people and mistreated our fathers, forcing them to expose their infants so that they would not be kept alive.

    Pinned source record

Exodus 1:11

He set overseers of labour over them to afflict them with their work. They built fortified cities for Pharaoh: Pithom, Rameses, and On, which is the City of the Sun.

  1. Exodus 5:6-14 Developed forced-labour account

    The later taskmaster and brick-quota narrative develops the coercive work regime introduced here, while its straw restrictions are not retroactively inserted into this first construction notice.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 5:6 Pharaoh commanded the people’s taskmasters and scribes:

    Exodus 5:7 “The people shall no longer be given straw for making bricks as before. Let them go and gather straw for themselves.

    Exodus 5:8 And you shall lay upon them the appointed number of bricks which they make each day; you shall not reduce it at all. For they are idle; therefore they cry out, saying, ‘Let us go and sacrifice to our God.’

    Exodus 5:9 Let these men’s tasks be heavier. Let them attend to them and pay no attention to empty words.”

    Exodus 5:10 The taskmasters and scribes pressed them and said to the people, “Thus says Pharaoh: ‘I will no longer give you straw.

    Exodus 5:11 Go yourselves, and gather straw for yourselves wherever you may find it; for your appointed number shall not be reduced.’”

    Exodus 5:12 The people scattered throughout the land of Egypt to gather stubble for straw.

    Exodus 5:13 The taskmasters pressed them, saying, “Complete the work due each day, as when straw was provided for you.”

    Exodus 5:14 And the scribes of the people of the sons of Israel, whom Pharaoh’s overseers had set over them, were scourged, and asked, “Why have you not completed your appointed number of bricks, as before, including that of today?”

    Pinned source record

  2. Deuteronomy 26:6 Explicit retrospective oppression

    The later confession says the Egyptians afflicted, humbled and imposed hard labours on Israel, recalling the overseers and afflicting work described here.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 26:6 And the Egyptians afflicted us, and humbled us, and laid hard labours upon us.

    Pinned source record

Exodus 1:12

But the more they humbled them, the more they multiplied and grew exceedingly, exceedingly strong; and the Egyptians loathed the sons of Israel.

  1. Exodus 1:7 Same-chapter intensified population repetition

    Israel's increase and exceptional strength repeat the earlier growth notice, now expressly continuing despite Egyptian humiliation rather than ceasing under it.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 1:7 And the sons of Israel increased and multiplied, and became numerous and exceedingly, exceedingly strong; and the land multiplied them.

    Pinned source record

  2. Psalms 104:24-25 Explicit retrospective growth-and-hostility account

    The psalm joins Israel's increase and strength with Egyptian hatred and deceit, corresponding to the conjunction of continued growth and loathing narrated here.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 104:24 He greatly increased His people and made them stronger than their enemies.

    Psalms 104:25 He turned their heart to hate His people, to deal deceitfully with His servants.

    Pinned source record

Exodus 1:13

The Egyptians oppressed the sons of Israel by force.

  1. Deuteronomy 26:6 Explicit retrospective oppression

    The later confession's hard labours and Egyptian humiliation recall the forcible oppression that this verse attributes to the Egyptians.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 26:6 And the Egyptians afflicted us, and humbled us, and laid hard labours upon us.

  2. Exodus 6:5 Oppression and divine response

    God later speaks of hearing the groaning of the Israelites whom Egypt enslaves, identifying the oppressive condition to which the renewed deliverance speech responds.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 6:5 I have heard the groaning of the sons of Israel, whom the Egyptians enslave, and I have remembered your covenant.

    Pinned source record

Exodus 1:14

They made their lives painful with hard labour in clay and brickmaking, and with all the work in the plains, in every task in which they enslaved them by force.

  1. Exodus 5:7-9 Developed brick-labour account

    The later refusal of straw, unchanged brick quotas and heavier tasks develop the brickmaking burden introduced here without making every task identical in both passages.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 5:7 “The people shall no longer be given straw for making bricks as before. Let them go and gather straw for themselves.

    Exodus 5:8 And you shall lay upon them the appointed number of bricks which they make each day; you shall not reduce it at all. For they are idle; therefore they cry out, saying, ‘Let us go and sacrifice to our God.’

    Exodus 5:9 Let these men’s tasks be heavier. Let them attend to them and pay no attention to empty words.”

    Pinned source record

  2. Deuteronomy 26:6 Explicit retrospective labour account

    The later historical confession recalls Egyptian affliction and hard labours, summarizing the painful clay, brick and field work described here.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 26:6 And the Egyptians afflicted us, and humbled us, and laid hard labours upon us.

Exodus 1:15

The king of the Egyptians spoke to the midwives of the Hebrews, one named Sepphora and the other Puah.

  1. Exodus 1:16-19 Same-chapter named participants and ensuing exchange

    The following command and questioning concern the midwives introduced here, identifying the women who resist the male-child order and answer the king; no identity with Moses' later wife is inferred from similar names.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 1:16 And he said, “When you attend the Hebrew women in childbirth, and they are about to give birth, if the child is male, kill him; but if the child is female, preserve her.”

    Exodus 1:17 But the midwives feared God. They did not do as the king of Egypt had commanded them, and they kept the boys alive.

    Exodus 1:18 And the king of Egypt called the midwives and said to them, “Why have you done this thing, and kept the male children alive?”

    Exodus 1:19 The midwives said to Pharaoh, “The Hebrew women are not like the Egyptian women, for they give birth before the midwives come to them.” And they bore children.

    Pinned source record

Exodus 1:16

And he said, “When you attend the Hebrew women in childbirth, and they are about to give birth, if the child is male, kill him; but if the child is female, preserve her.”

  1. Exodus 1:22 Same-chapter expanded killing order

    The later command extends the male-child killing policy to all Pharaoh's people and specifies the river, preserving the distinct midwife instruction and wider decree.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 1:22 And Pharaoh commanded all his people, saying, “Cast every male child born to the Hebrews into the river, but keep every female child alive.”

Exodus 1:17

But the midwives feared God. They did not do as the king of Egypt had commanded them, and they kept the boys alive.

  1. Exodus 1:20-21 Same-chapter fear of God and ensuing treatment

    The account next reports God's good treatment of the midwives and repeats their fear of God; the house-making clause retains its supplied agency rather than being rewritten as God building their houses.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 1:20 God dealt well with the midwives, and the people multiplied and became very strong.

    Exodus 1:21 And because the midwives feared God, they made houses for themselves.

    Pinned source record

  2. Acts 5:29 Related obedience principle

    The apostles' declaration that God must be obeyed rather than humans provides a later expression of the priority reflected in the midwives' fear of God and refusal of the king's killing command.

    Read matching passage

    Acts 5:29 Peter and the apostles answered, “We must obey God rather than human beings.

Exodus 1:22

And Pharaoh commanded all his people, saying, “Cast every male child born to the Hebrews into the river, but keep every female child alive.”

  1. Exodus 2:3 Royal decree and endangered-child setting

    The river decree supplies the danger surrounding the mother's placement of her male child in a protected basket in the river marsh; her action is not equated with carrying out the killing order.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 2:3 When they could no longer hide him, his mother took a basket for him and coated it with bitumen-pitch. She put the child in it and placed it in the marsh beside the river.
